<table>

<td valign="top"><strong>Question:</strong> For the task of identifying this protein’s signal peptide, which description best fits the sequence?<br><br><strong>Choices:</strong><br><br><strong>A.</strong> An internal hydrophobic segment that anchors the protein in a membrane<br><br><strong>B.</strong> A C-terminal region that directs export from the cell<br><br><strong>C.</strong> An N-terminal domain that forms part of the mature protein’s catalytic core<br><br><strong>D.</strong> An N-terminal leader that functions as a signal peptide<br><br><strong>Flash-Next response:</strong><br><br><br><br>1. reasoning: Analyzing the N-terminal region of the protein sequence: MKSKGSIMACLILFSFTITTFINTETISAFSNQ...<br><br>A signal peptide has three characteristic regions:<br>- **n-region**: Positively charged residues at the very N-terminus. Here we see &quot;MKSKG&quot; with lysine (K) providing positive charge.<br>- **h-region**: A hydrophobic core. Here we see &quot;SIMACLILFSFTITTF&quot; — a long stretch of hydrophobic residues (I, M, A, C, L, I, L, F, S, F, T, I, T, T, F).<br>- **c-region**: A more polar region with the cleavage site, often containing small residues (A, S, G) at positions -1 and -3. Here we see &quot;INTETISAFSNQ&quot; which provides the polar context and potential cleavage site.<br><br>Evaluating each option:<br>- **A**: Describes a transmembrane anchor, which is internal and typically not cleaved. Signal peptides are N-terminal and are cleaved off. Incorrect.<br>- **B**: Signal peptides are N-terminal, not C-terminal. The CXSC motif (or CxxC where x represents amino acids) is typically a redox-active disulfide that can form and break during catalytic cycles. This is characteristic of proteins that use disulfide exchange in their function.

Given that:
1. The cysteines forming the disulfide (C-L-S-C) are within the same polypeptide
2. The CxxC/CXSC motif is characteristic of redox-active disulfides
3. This appears to be a single polypeptide chain

The best answer would be B: A redox-active disulfide linking two cysteines within the same polypeptide.

Let me also consider the other options:
- A (inter-molecular stable structural): Unlikely because the CXSC motif suggests redox activity, not structural stability
- C (metal-binding): CXSC can sometimes bind metals (like zinc), but in the context of this protein family (appears to be a phosphatase), the disulfide is more likely redox-active
- D (redox-active but inter-molecular): The cysteines are clearly in the same polypeptide sequence, not separate chains

So the answer is B.
</pre>
